---
title: "Flow matching and rectified flow"
description: "Generating data by integrating a learned velocity field dx/dt = v(x, t) from noise to data: continuous normalizing flows, flow matching and the conditional flow-matching loss (Lipman et al.), rectified flow with straight paths, target x₁ − x₀, marginal preservation and reflow (Liu et al.), stochastic interpolants as the common frame, why Gaussian flow matching is diffusion in other coordinates (the velocity is affine in the score; v-prediction), Stable Diffusion 3's logit-normal timestep sampling and resolution shift, and a NumPy rectified flow trained from scratch on two moons, sampled with 1–100 Euler steps before and after one reflow."

*Beyond the lectures · [[8-diffusion-models/index|Diffusion models]] · sourced from Chen et al. (2018), Lipman et al. (2023), Liu et al. (2023), Albergo & Vanden-Eijnden (2023), Albergo et al. (2025), Salimans & Ho (2022) and Esser et al. (2024) · builds on [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|the probability-flow ODE]] and [[2-calculus-and-optimization/gradient-descent|gradient descent]]*

> [!abstract] In one breath
> Instead of defining a noising SDE and learning to reverse it, pick a path from noise to data directly — the simplest is a straight line, $x_t=t\,x_1+(1-t)\,x_0$ with $x_0$ noise and $x_1$ data — and train a network $v_\theta(x,t)$ by plain regression onto the line's velocity $x_1-x_0$. The regression's optimum is a velocity field whose ODE $\mathrm dx/\mathrm dt=v(x,t)$ carries the noise distribution exactly onto the data distribution, so sampling is "start from noise, take Euler steps". Where many lines cross, the learned paths curve; **reflow** retrains on the model's own (noise, sample) pairs and straightens them until one or two steps suffice. With Gaussian noise this is diffusion in other coordinates — the velocity is an affine function of the score, and the ODE is the probability-flow ODE — which is why Stable Diffusion 3, FLUX and most current image generators could switch to it without changing anything else.

## What it is

A **continuous normalizing flow** (CNF; Chen et al., 2018) generates data by moving points with a time-dependent velocity field $v_t:\mathbb R^d\to\mathbb R^d$:

$$
\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\phi_t(x)=v_t\big(\phi_t(x)\big),\qquad \phi_0(x)=x .
$$

If $x\sim p_0$ (noise), then $\phi_t(x)$ has some distribution $p_t$, the **push-forward** of $p_0$ by the flow, and the densities obey the continuity equation $\partial_t p_t+\nabla\!\cdot(p_t\,v_t)=0$ — probability is conserved as it moves. A velocity field "generates" a path of densities $p_t$ when this holds. The generative question is: find $v$ such that $p_1$ is the data distribution. Chen et al. trained CNFs by maximum likelihood, which needs an ODE solve inside every training step. Flow matching removes the simulation from training.

> [!definition] Definition — flow matching
> Choose a path of distributions $p_t$ from $p_0=\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ to $p_1\approx q$ (the data), and let $u_t$ be a velocity field that generates it. **Flow matching** regresses a network onto that field,
> $$
> \mathcal L_{\text{FM}}(\theta)=\mathbb E_{t\sim\mathcal U[0,1],\;x\sim p_t}\big\|v_\theta(x,t)-u_t(x)\big\|^2 ,
> $$
> and samples by integrating $\mathrm dx/\mathrm dt=v_\theta(x,t)$ from $t=0$ to $t=1$. **Rectified flow** is the special case of straight paths between noise and data samples, with regression target $x_1-x_0$.

**Time convention on this page:** noise at $t=0$, data at $t=1$ (Lipman et al., Liu et al.). Stable Diffusion 3 uses the opposite (data at $t=0$); see [[8-diffusion-models/flow-matching-and-rectified-flow#flow-matching-is-diffusion-in-other-coordinates|the table of conventions]] below.

## What you should know

- A CNF moves samples with an ODE $\dot x=v_t(x)$; the density follows the continuity equation. Sampling = numerical ODE integration (Euler: $x\leftarrow x+h\,v_\theta(x,t)$).
- **Conditional flow matching** (Lipman et al., 2023): the marginal field $u_t(x)$ is unknown, but regressing onto a *conditional* field $u_t(x\mid x_1)$ for paths ending at a single data point gives the **same gradients** (Thm. 2).
- Gaussian conditional paths $\mathcal N(\mu_t(x_1),\sigma_t^2\mathbf I)$ have $u_t(x\mid x_1)=\tfrac{\sigma_t'}{\sigma_t}(x-\mu_t)+\mu_t'$; the **OT path** $\mu_t=t\,x_1$, $\sigma_t=1-(1-\sigma_{\min})t$ gives the target $x_1-(1-\sigma_{\min})x_0$.
- **Rectified flow** (Liu et al., 2023): $X_t=tX_1+(1-t)X_0$, loss $\int_0^1\mathbb E\,\|(X_1-X_0)-v(X_t,t)\|^2\,\mathrm dt$, optimum $v(x,t)=\mathbb E[X_1-X_0\mid X_t=x]$. The ODE preserves every marginal (Thm. 3.3).
- The learned paths **curve** where straight lines cross, because the optimum averages over all lines through a point; ODE paths themselves never cross.
- One Euler step of a fresh rectified flow from $t=0$ lands every sample on the **data mean**: $v(z,0)=\mathbb E[X_1]-z$.
- **Reflow** retrains on pairs $(Z_0,Z_1)$ produced by the flow itself; it keeps the marginals, lowers transport cost and straightens paths, allowing one-step sampling.
- **Gaussian flow matching is diffusion:** with $x_t=\alpha_tx_1+\sigma_t\varepsilon$, $u=\tfrac{\dot\alpha_t}{\alpha_t}x+\big(\tfrac{\dot\alpha_t\sigma_t^2}{\alpha_t}-\dot\sigma_t\sigma_t\big)\nabla\log p_t(x)$. Predicting $\varepsilon$, $x_1$, $v$ or the score are affine re-parameterizations of each other; **v-prediction** is velocity prediction on a circular path.
- **SD3** trains rectified flow with **logit-normal** timesteps, $t=\operatorname{sigmoid}(u)$, $u\sim\mathcal N(m,s^2)$ (best: $m=0,s=1$), and shifts timesteps towards noise at high resolution.

## From a probability path to a regression target

Lipman et al. (2023) build the path from pieces. For each data point $x_1$, choose a **conditional path** $p_t(x\mid x_1)$ that starts at the noise, $p_0(x\mid x_1)=\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$, and ends concentrated at $x_1$, $p_1(x\mid x_1)=\mathcal N(x_1,\sigma_{\min}^2\mathbf I)$, together with a field $u_t(x\mid x_1)$ that generates it. Averaging over the data gives the **marginal path** and the **marginal field**

$$
p_t(x)=\int p_t(x\mid x_1)\,q(x_1)\,\mathrm dx_1,\qquad
u_t(x)=\int u_t(x\mid x_1)\,\frac{p_t(x\mid x_1)\,q(x_1)}{p_t(x)}\,\mathrm dx_1 ,
$$

and Theorem 1 says this $u_t$ generates $p_t$: the marginal field is the posterior-weighted average of the conditional ones. $p_1$ is the data blurred by $\sigma_{\min}$, so integrating $u_t$ from noise produces (almost) the data.

$u_t(x)$ involves an integral over the whole data set and cannot be evaluated. The fix is the same as for [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|denoising score matching]]: regress on the conditional quantity instead.

> [!key] Theorem — conditional flow matching (Lipman et al., 2023, Thm. 2)
> $$
> \mathcal L_{\text{CFM}}(\theta)=\mathbb E_{t,\;x_1\sim q,\;x\sim p_t(x\mid x_1)}\big\|v_\theta(x,t)-u_t(x\mid x_1)\big\|^2
> $$
> differs from $\mathcal L_{\text{FM}}$ by a constant independent of $\theta$, so $\nabla_\theta\mathcal L_{\text{CFM}}=\nabla_\theta\mathcal L_{\text{FM}}$. The regression's minimizer is the marginal field $u_t$, because the minimizer of a squared error is a conditional expectation: $v^*(x,t)=\mathbb E[u_t(x\mid x_1)\mid x_t=x]$.

**Gaussian paths.** For $p_t(x\mid x_1)=\mathcal N(\mu_t(x_1),\sigma_t(x_1)^2\mathbf I)$ the simplest generating flow is $\psi_t(x_0)=\sigma_t x_0+\mu_t$ with $x_0\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$, and its velocity, written as a function of the current point, is (Thm. 3)

$$
u_t(x\mid x_1)=\frac{\sigma_t'}{\sigma_t}\,\big(x-\mu_t\big)+\mu_t' .
$$

Diffusion's noising paths are Gaussian paths of this kind (Lipman et al., Sec. 4.1, with time reversed so that noise is at $t=0$). The **optimal-transport (OT) path** instead moves mean and standard deviation linearly, $\mu_t=t\,x_1$, $\sigma_t=1-(1-\sigma_{\min})t$, so that $\psi_t(x_0)=(1-(1-\sigma_{\min})t)\,x_0+t\,x_1$ and the loss becomes

$$
\mathcal L_{\text{CFM}}(\theta)=\mathbb E_{t,\;x_1\sim q,\;x_0\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)}\Big\|v_\theta\big(\psi_t(x_0),t\big)-\big(x_1-(1-\sigma_{\min})\,x_0\big)\Big\|^2 .
$$

Each conditional path is a straight line traversed at constant speed. With $\sigma_{\min}=0$ this is exactly the rectified-flow loss below.

## Rectified flow

Liu, Gong & Liu (2023) start from any **coupling** $(X_0,X_1)$ of two distributions — for generation, $X_0\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ and $X_1\sim$ data, drawn independently — and the straight interpolation $X_t=tX_1+(1-t)X_0$. They fit the velocity of these lines,

$$
\min_v\int_0^1\mathbb E\Big[\big\|(X_1-X_0)-v(X_t,t)\big\|^2\Big]\,\mathrm dt,\qquad v^X(x,t)=\mathbb E\big[X_1-X_0\mid X_t=x\big],
$$

and call the ODE $\mathrm dZ_t=v^X(Z_t,t)\,\mathrm dt$, $Z_0=X_0$, the **rectified flow** of the coupling.

> [!key] Properties (Liu et al., 2023)
> - **Marginals are preserved** (Thm. 3.3): $Z_t$ has the same distribution as $X_t$ for every $t$; in particular $Z_1$ is distributed as the data.
> - **Transport cost does not increase** (Sec. 3): $\mathbb E[c(Z_1-Z_0)]\le\mathbb E[c(X_1-X_0)]$ for every convex cost $c$.
> - **Paths do not cross.** $Z_t$ solves an ODE, so two trajectories cannot meet. The straight lines $X_t$ do cross; at a crossing point $v^X$ averages their directions, and the trajectories bend there instead of crossing.

**Reflow.** The coupling $(Z_0,Z_1)$ produced by a rectified flow is deterministic — $Z_1$ is a function of $Z_0$ — and its straight lines cross far less than those of the independent coupling. Training a new rectified flow on it, $Z^{k+1}=\text{Rectflow}\big((Z_0^k,Z_1^k)\big)$, keeps the marginals and makes the paths straighter. Straightness is measured by

$$
S(Z)=\int_0^1\mathbb E\Big[\big\|(Z_1-Z_0)-\dot Z_t\big\|^2\Big]\,\mathrm dt ,
$$

which is zero exactly for straight, constant-speed paths — and then one Euler step $Z_1=Z_0+v(Z_0,0)$ is exact. Theorem 3.7 bounds it: the straightest of the first $K$ rectified flows has $S\le\mathbb E\|X_1-X_0\|^2/K$. In practice one or two reflows suffice, optionally followed by distilling the $k$-rectified flow into a one-step model. The price is a full sampling run to generate the training pairs, and, as Sauer et al. (2024) note, training on an earlier model's outputs accumulates its errors.

**Why one step of a fresh flow fails.** At $t=0$, $X_t=X_0$ is independent of $X_1$, so $v^X(z,0)=\mathbb E[X_1-X_0\mid X_0=z]=\mathbb E[X_1]-z$, and one Euler step gives $z+v^X(z,0)=\mathbb E[X_1]$ for **every** $z$: all samples land on the data mean. The toy below shows exactly that.

## A rectified flow from scratch, in NumPy

The data are 20,000 points from the same two-moons generator as the [[8-diffusion-models/ddpm|DDPM]] toy (scaled to about unit variance); the noise is $\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ in 2-D. The velocity network is a small MLP on $[x,\,t,\,\sin 2\pi kt,\,\cos 2\pi kt]_{k=1..4}$ (11 inputs, three hidden layers of 128 SiLU units, 2 outputs, about 35k parameters), with hand-written [[2-calculus-and-optimization/backpropagation|backpropagation]] and Adam. Everything below was run; the code is `tools/flow_toy.py` and `tools/figs_diff_c.py`.

```python
NF = 4                                      # Fourier features of t

def features(x, t):
    """Input: position plus Fourier features of t."""
    k = 2 * np.pi * np.arange(1, NF + 1)
    tt = t[:, None] * k[None, :]
    return np.concatenate([x, t[:, None], np.sin(tt), np.cos(tt)], axis=1)
```

Training is the rectified-flow regression, one minibatch of pairs and one random time per sample:

```python
def train(pairs, rng, steps=6000, batch=512, lr=2e-3, params=None):
    """Rectified-flow regression on a coupling (X0, X1).

    pairs: function(n, rng) -> (x0, x1), each of shape (n, 2).
    """
    if params is None:
        params = init_params(rng)
    state = {"t": 0, "m": [np.zeros_like(p) for p in params],
             "v": [np.zeros_like(p) for p in params]}
    losses = []
    for step in range(steps):
        x0, x1 = pairs(batch, rng)
        t = rng.uniform(0, 1, batch)
        xt = t[:, None] * x1 + (1 - t[:, None]) * x0   # straight path
        target = x1 - x0                               # constant velocity
        v, cache = forward(params, xt, t)
        err = v - target
        losses.append(np.mean(np.sum(err ** 2, axis=1)))
        dout = 2 * err / batch                         # d(mean ||err||^2)
        grads = backward(params, cache, dout)
        a = lr * 0.5 * (1 + np.cos(np.pi * step / steps))  # cosine decay
        adam_step(params, grads, state, a)
    return params, np.array(losses)
```

`pairs` is either `independent_pairs()` (noise and a random data point, for the 1-rectified flow) or `fixed_pairs(z0, z1)` (a stored coupling, for reflow). Sampling is Euler on the ODE:

```python
def euler(params, z0, n_steps, keep_path=False):
    """Integrate dZ/dt = v(Z, t) from t = 0 to 1."""
    z = z0.copy()
    path = [z.copy()]
    dt = 1.0 / n_steps
    for k in range(n_steps):
        t = np.full(len(z), k * dt)
        v, _ = forward(params, z, t)
        z = z + dt * v
        if keep_path:
            path.append(z.copy())
    return (z, np.array(path)) if keep_path else z
```

and the experiment trains a 1-rectified flow, generates 20,000 (noise, sample) pairs with it, and trains the 2-rectified flow on them, starting from the 1-rectified weights:

```python
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
p1, l1 = ft.train(ft.independent_pairs(), rng, steps=6000)
test = ft.two_moons(4000, np.random.default_rng(7))
z0 = np.random.default_rng(3).standard_normal((4000, 2))
er = np.random.default_rng(11)
steps = [1, 2, 5, 20, 100]
sw1 = [ft.sliced_w1(ft.euler(p1, z0, n), test, er) for n in steps]
s1 = ft.straightness(p1, z0)
zz0 = np.random.default_rng(5).standard_normal((20000, 2))
zz1 = ft.euler(p1, zz0, 100)
p2, l2 = ft.train(ft.fixed_pairs(zz0, zz1), rng, steps=6000, params=[p.copy() for p in p1])
```

height="123.48"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f82fb28"><rect x="158.03" y="169.73" width="114.03" height="123.48"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f82fb29"><rect x="294.86" y="169.73" width="114.03" height="123.48"/></clipPath><clipPath id="f82fb2a"><rect x="431.69" y="169.73" width="114.03" height="123.48"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Few-step sampling of a learned flow.</b> Top: the 1-rectified flow (trained on independent noise–data pairs) integrated with 1, 2, 5 and 20 Euler steps; grey is real data. With one step every sample lands near the data mean, because at t = 0 the learned velocity is roughly the average direction to the data. Bottom: after one reflow (retraining on the flow's own noise → sample pairs) the paths are nearly straight and a single Euler step already gives the two moons.</figcaption></figure>

y="21.56" width="202.91" height="232.88"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>ODE trajectories from noise to data</b>, integrated with 100 Euler steps from 60 noise samples (small dots in the text colour). Left: the 1-rectified flow; paths bend because the marginal velocity averages over all straight noise–data lines passing through a point. Right: after one reflow the coupling is (nearly) deterministic and the paths are (nearly) straight. Measured straightness ∫E‖(Z₁ − Z₀) − v‖² dt drops from 0.91 to 0.00017.</figcaption></figure>

Sample quality is measured by the sliced Wasserstein-1 distance (the average over 128 random directions of the 1-D Wasserstein distance between the projected point clouds) from 4,000 samples to 4,000 held-out data points. Two independent data samples are 0.014 apart — the floor — and raw noise is 0.215 from the data.

| Euler steps | 1 | 2 | 5 | 20 | 100 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1-rectified flow | 0.785 | 0.380 | 0.134 | 0.028 | 0.013 |
| 2-rectified flow (one reflow) | 0.018 | 0.015 | 0.014 | 0.013 | 0.013 |

the dashed line is the distance between two independent data samples, i.e. the noise floor). The reflowed model reaches the floor with one or two steps; the 1-rectified flow needs about 20.</figcaption></figure>

**Reading the results.**

- **One step of the 1-rectified flow is worse than no model at all** (0.785 against 0.215 for raw noise): every sample lands near the data mean, as derived above. It needs about 20 steps to get close to the floor.
- **After one reflow, one step suffices** (0.018, against a floor of 0.014). Straightness $S$, computed along 100-step trajectories, drops from 0.912 to 0.00017.
- **The losses tell the same story.** The final training loss of the 1-rectified flow is 2.74, far from zero, because $X_1-X_0$ is not a function of $X_t$: many lines pass through each point, and the best a regression can do is their average. On the reflow coupling the lines hardly cross, the target is (almost) a function of $X_t$, and the loss falls to 0.00023.
- With many steps both flows reach the floor (0.013): reflow does not improve the distribution, it makes it cheap to reach.

## Flow matching is diffusion in other coordinates

For any Gaussian path $x_t=\alpha_t\,x_1+\sigma_t\,\varepsilon$ (here $\alpha_0=0,\sigma_0=1$ and $\alpha_1=1,\sigma_1=0$), the marginal velocity is $u(x,t)=\mathbb E[\dot\alpha_tx_1+\dot\sigma_t\varepsilon\mid x_t=x]$. The posterior mean of the noise is the scaled score — the statement "[[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|predicting the noise is estimating the score]]", $\mathbb E[\varepsilon\mid x_t=x]=-\sigma_t\nabla\log p_t(x)$ — and $\mathbb E[x_1\mid x]=(x-\sigma_t\,\mathbb E[\varepsilon\mid x])/\alpha_t$. Substituting,

$$
u(x,t)=\frac{\dot\alpha_t}{\alpha_t}\,x+\Big(\frac{\dot\alpha_t\,\sigma_t^2}{\alpha_t}-\dot\sigma_t\,\sigma_t\Big)\nabla_x\log p_t(x),
\qquad\text{straight path: } u(x,t)=\frac{x+(1-t)\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)}{t}.
$$

So the learned velocity is an **affine function of the score**, and the flow ODE is a probability-flow ODE. For the variance-preserving path, $\alpha=e^{-\frac12\int\beta}$ and $\sigma=\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}$ in diffusion time, the same formula gives exactly the drift $-\tfrac12\beta(t)\,\big[x+\nabla\log p_t(x)\big]$ of the [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching#the-probability-flow-ode|probability-flow ODE]]. (`tools/figs_diff_c.py` checks the straight-path relation by Monte Carlo, in SD3's time convention.) What flow matching changes is the **path** (straight instead of the VP curve), the **parameterization** of the network output, and the **weighting** of the loss over $t$ — not the kind of object being learned.

The parameterizations are interchangeable given $(x_t,t)$. On the straight path,

$$
\hat x_1=x_t+(1-t)\,v,\qquad \hat\varepsilon=x_t-t\,v,\qquad v=\frac{x_t-\hat\varepsilon}{t},
$$

so a velocity model is also a denoiser and a noise predictor; the choice changes how errors at different $t$ are weighted, not the optimum (Esser et al., 2024, Sec. 2).

**v-prediction** (Salimans & Ho, 2022) is the same idea from the diffusion side: with $z=\alpha_t x+\sigma_t\varepsilon$ the network predicts $v\equiv\alpha_t\varepsilon-\sigma_t x$. On the cosine path $\alpha=\cos\phi$, $\sigma=\sin\phi$ this is exactly the velocity $\mathrm dz/\mathrm d\phi=\cos\phi\,\varepsilon-\sin\phi\,x$: v-prediction is velocity prediction along a quarter circle (data at $\phi=0$, noise at $\phi=\pi/2$) instead of a straight line.

| method | path | network predicts | data at |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DDPM / score SDE | $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon$ | $\varepsilon$ (a scaled score) | $t=0$ |
| v-prediction, cosine | $\cos\phi\,x+\sin\phi\,\varepsilon$ | $\cos\phi\,\varepsilon-\sin\phi\,x$ | $\phi=0$ |
| flow matching, OT path, $\sigma_{\min}=0$ | $t\,x_1+(1-t)\,x_0$ | $x_1-x_0$ | $t=1$ |
| rectified flow (Liu et al.) | $tX_1+(1-t)X_0$ | $X_1-X_0$ | $t=1$ |
| SD3 (Esser et al.) | $(1-t)\,x_0+t\,\varepsilon$ | $\varepsilon-x_0$ | $t=0$ |
| Qwen-Image report | $t\,x_0+(1-t)\,x_1$, $x_1$ noise | $x_0-x_1$ | $t=1$ |

The last two rows describe the same straight path; only the names of the endpoints and the direction of $t$ differ. Always check a paper's convention before copying a formula.

## Stochastic interpolants: the common frame

Albergo & Vanden-Eijnden (2023) proposed the same construction independently: pick an **interpolant** $x_t=I(t,x_0,x_1)$ between samples of two distributions, with $I(0,x_0,x_1)=x_0$ and $I(1,x_0,x_1)=x_1$, and learn the velocity $b(t,x)=\mathbb E[\partial_tI\mid x_t=x]$ by a quadratic regression. Albergo, Boffi & Vanden-Eijnden (2025) add a latent noise term, $x_t=I(t,x_0,x_1)+\gamma(t)\,z$ with $\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=0$, and show that the density of $x_t$ satisfies a transport equation **and** a family of Fokker–Planck equations with tunable diffusion coefficient. Consequently the same interpolant yields both a deterministic generator (an ODE, as in flow matching) and stochastic ones (SDEs with an adjustable amount of noise), with drifts obtained as minimizers of simple quadratic objectives — one of them a new objective for the score.

In this frame diffusion, flow matching and rectified flow are choices of $I$, $\gamma$ and the endpoint distributions; neither endpoint has to be Gaussian, so the same machinery maps one data distribution to another. The SDE/ODE pair of the [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|reverse-time page]] is the special case of a Gaussian $x_0$.

## Why Stable Diffusion 3 switched, and how it samples $t$

Esser et al. (2024) compared diffusion and flow formulations for text-to-image latent models under one framework and trained SD3 with rectified flow: $z_t=(1-t)\,x_0+t\,\varepsilon$ (data at $t=0$), network output = velocity, target $\varepsilon-x_0$. Two observations drive their design.

**Not all $t$ are equally hard.** Near either end the optimal prediction is essentially a mean — at $t=0$ the target $\varepsilon-x_0$ is the known $-x_0$ plus the noise mean, at $t=1$ it is the known noise minus the data mean — while in the middle it is genuinely hard (Sec. 3.1). Sampling $t$ from a density $\pi(t)$ instead of uniformly is the same as weighting the loss by $\tfrac{t}{1-t}\pi(t)$ (eq. 18), so SD3 samples more intermediate times with the **logit-normal** density (eq. 19)

$$
\pi_{\text{ln}}(t;m,s)=\frac{1}{s\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\frac{1}{t(1-t)}\exp\!\Big(-\frac{(\operatorname{logit}t-m)^2}{2s^2}\Big),\qquad \operatorname{logit}t=\log\frac{t}{1-t},
$$

drawn as $t=\operatorname{sigmoid}(u)$ with $u\sim\mathcal N(m,s^2)$. $m<0$ biases training towards data, $m>0$ towards noise.

(2024), eq. 19: t = sigmoid(u) with u drawn from a normal distribution N(m, s²). Compared with uniform t, the logit-normal puts more training weight on intermediate noise levels and almost none at the two ends; a positive m shifts it towards noise.</figcaption></figure>

In their ranking of formulations over two datasets and several sampler settings (Tab. 1), **rf/lognorm(0.00, 1.00)** has the best average rank (1.54), ahead of the established latent-diffusion baseline *eps/linear* (2.88) and far ahead of rectified flow with uniform $t$ (5.67). Rectified flows also degrade least when sampled with few steps; with 25 or more steps only rf/lognorm(0.00, 1.00) stays competitive with eps/linear (Fig. 3).

**Higher resolutions need more noise.** With more pixels, the same noise level leaves more information about the image (the average of $n$ noisy pixels estimates their common value with error $\propto1/\sqrt n$). SD3 therefore maps timesteps towards the noise end at resolution $m$ pixels relative to $n$ (eq. 23),

$$
t_m=\frac{\alpha\,t_n}{1+(\alpha-1)\,t_n},\qquad \alpha=\sqrt{m/n},
$$

and uses $\alpha=3.0$ for training and sampling at 1024×1024, after a human-preference study preferred shifts above 1.5.

SD3 uses α = 3.0 at 1024×1024.</figcaption></figure>

In PyTorch, one SD3-style training step is a few lines (a sketch; `model` and the latent shapes are placeholders):

```python
eps = torch.randn_like(x0)                     # x0: (B, 16, h, w) latents
t = torch.sigmoid(torch.randn(B, device=x0.device))  # logit-normal(0, 1)
tt = t[:, None, None, None]
z_t = (1 - tt) * x0 + tt * eps                 # SD3: data at t = 0
loss = F.mse_loss(model(z_t, t, text), eps - x0)   # velocity target
```

[`torch.randn`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn.html) and [`torch.sigmoid`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.sigmoid.html) give the logit-normal times; the loss is plain [`F.mse_loss`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.functional.mse_loss.html). The sampling loop that goes with it is on [[8-diffusion-models/modern-image-generators#how-a-prompt-becomes-an-image|the next page]].

> [!trap] Common mistakes
> - **Mixing time conventions.** Liu and Lipman put noise at $t=0$; SD3 puts data at $t=0$. The velocity's sign and the direction of integration flip with the convention: in SD3's convention the sampler runs from $t=1$ down to $0$ and the update is $z\leftarrow z+(t_{i+1}-t_i)\,v$ with $t_{i+1}<t_i$.
> - **Expecting the training loss to go to zero.** On independent pairs the target $X_1-X_0$ is not a function of $X_t$; the loss has an irreducible part (2.74 in the toy). It is the regression optimum, not underfitting.
> - **"Straight training paths mean straight sampling paths."** Only the conditional paths are straight. The marginal field averages them, and the learned trajectories curve; one-step sampling needs reflow or distillation.
> - **Thinking flow matching is a different kind of model from diffusion.** With Gaussian noise the velocity is an affine function of the score and the ODE is a probability-flow ODE; the samplers of the [[8-diffusion-models/fast-sampling-ddim-and-ode-solvers|fast-sampling page]] apply.
> - **Uniform $t$ by default.** For image models SD3 found uniform $t$ clearly worse than logit-normal $t$.
> - **Forgetting the resolution.** A schedule tuned at 256×256 under-noises 1024×1024 images; SD3 shifts $t$ with $\alpha=\sqrt{m/n}$.

> [!question]- Check yourself
> 1. Write the rectified-flow loss for one pair $(x_0,x_1)$ and one time $t$. What is the network's input and what is its target?
> 2. Why can't the learned trajectories of a rectified flow cross, although the training lines do?
> 3. Show that one Euler step from $t=0$ of the optimal 1-rectified flow maps every noise sample to $\mathbb E[X_1]$. Which number in the toy's table shows it?
> 4. After reflow the toy's training loss drops from 2.74 to 0.00023. Why?
> 5. On the straight path $x_t=t\,x_1+(1-t)\,\varepsilon$, a network predicts $v=0.5$ (1-D) at $x_t=0.2$, $t=0.6$. What are $\hat x_1$ and $\hat\varepsilon$?
> 6. Under SD3's logit-normal(0, 1), what is the median training time $t$? What does $m=0.5$ do?
> 7. With shift $\alpha=3$, where does the base-resolution time $t_n=0.5$ go?
>
> > [!solution]- Answers
> > 1. $\big\|v_\theta(t\,x_1+(1-t)\,x_0,\;t)-(x_1-x_0)\big\|^2$. Input: the interpolated point and $t$; target: the constant velocity $x_1-x_0$ of the line.
> > 2. They are solutions of one ODE $\dot z=v(z,t)$ with a (Lipschitz) field; two solutions that met would coincide forever after, by uniqueness. Where lines cross, $v$ is the average of their directions and the trajectories bend.
> > 3. At $t=0$, $X_t=X_0$ carries no information about the independent $X_1$, so $v(z,0)=\mathbb E[X_1]-z$ and $z+v(z,0)=\mathbb E[X_1]$. The toy's 1-step error 0.785 is worse than raw noise (0.215): all samples sit near the mean.
> > 4. The reflow coupling is deterministic and its lines rarely cross, so $X_1-X_0$ is (almost) a function of $X_t$ and the regression can fit it exactly; on independent pairs it cannot.
> > 5. $\hat x_1=x_t+(1-t)v=0.2+0.4\cdot0.5=0.4$ and $\hat\varepsilon=x_t-t\,v=0.2-0.6\cdot0.5=-0.1$. Check: $0.6\cdot0.4+0.4\cdot(-0.1)=0.2$.
> > 6. $t=\operatorname{sigmoid}(0)=0.5$ (the median of $u$ is $m=0$). $m=0.5$ moves the median to $\operatorname{sigmoid}(0.5)\approx0.62$, towards noise in SD3's convention.
> > 7. $t_m=3\cdot0.5/(1+2\cdot0.5)=0.75$: three-quarters of the way to pure noise.

## References

- Chen, Rubanova, Bettencourt, Duvenaud (2018). *Neural Ordinary Differential Equations.* NeurIPS 2018. [arXiv:1806.07366](https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.07366) — continuous normalizing flows.
- Lipman, Chen, Ben-Hamu, Nickel, Le (2023). *Flow Matching for Generative Modeling.* ICLR 2023. [arXiv:2210.02747](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.02747) — Thms. 1–3, Gaussian and OT conditional paths (Sec. 4).
- Liu, Gong, Liu (2023). *Flow Straight and Fast: Learning to Generate and Transfer Data with Rectified Flow.* ICLR 2023. [arXiv:2209.03003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.03003) — rectified flow, marginal preservation (Thm. 3.3), transport cost, straightness (Thm. 3.7), reflow.
- Albergo, Vanden-Eijnden (2023). *Building Normalizing Flows with Stochastic Interpolants.* ICLR 2023. [arXiv:2209.15571](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.15571)
- Albergo, Boffi, Vanden-Eijnden (2025). *Stochastic Interpolants: A Unifying Framework for Flows and Diffusions.* JMLR. [arXiv:2303.08797](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.08797)
- Salimans, Ho (2022). *Progressive Distillation for Fast Sampling of Diffusion Models.* ICLR 2022. [arXiv:2202.00512](https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.00512) — v-prediction.
- Esser et al. (2024). *Scaling Rectified Flow Transformers for High-Resolution Image Synthesis* (Stable Diffusion 3). ICML 2024. [arXiv:2403.03206](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.03206) — eqs. 13, 18, 19, 23; Tab. 1; Figs. 3 and 6.
- Sauer, Boesel, Dockhorn, Blattmann, Esser, Rombach (2024). *Fast High-Resolution Image Synthesis with Latent Adversarial Diffusion Distillation.* [arXiv:2403.12015](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.12015) — on reflow's error accumulation.
- Qwen Team (2025). *Qwen-Image Technical Report.* [arXiv:2508.02324](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.02324) — eq. 1 (its time convention), logit-normal $t$.

## PyTorch docs

- [`torch.randn`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn.html) and [`torch.randn_like`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn_like.html) — noise samples and logit-normal draws.
- [`torch.sigmoid`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.sigmoid.html) — maps $u\sim\mathcal N(m,s^2)$ to $t\in(0,1)$.
- [`torch.nn.functional.mse_loss`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.functional.mse_loss.html) — the flow-matching regression.
- [`torch.optim.Adam`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.optim.Adam.html) and [`torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR.html) — what the toy's hand-written optimizer and cosine decay correspond to.
